[tool-fff] exec_command <- Parse FPbase CSV and map PDB IDs to FPbase entries ($ cd /tmp && python3 << 'EOF' import csv pdb_ids = ['2IB5','4KGE','4Q7T','1KFD','5JZL','6AA7','3ADF','5WJ2','3M22','2H5Q','3MGF','5Y01','2VZX','4CQH','1YZW','2CHA','3KZY','1AK4','5P21','1H6R'] rows = list(csv.DictReader(open('fp_all.csv'))) print("rows:", len(rows)) # Each row has 'pdb', 'pdb.0'..'pdb.9' hits = {} for r in rows: pdb_fields = [] for k,v in r.items(): if k.startswith('pdb') and v: pdb_fields.append(v) # find which of our pdb_ids appear found = [pid for pid in pdb_ids if pid in pdb_fields or pid.lower() in [x.lower() for x in pdb_fields]] if found: hits.setdefault(tuple(found), []).append((r['name'], r['slug'], pdb_fields)) for k,v in hits.items(): print(k, '->', v) EOF)

The antibody might be "anti-GFP" antibody. In that case, the antibody binder sequence = GFP (EGFP, Aequorea victoria). But GFP sequences are in pdb_ids (1H6R, 5JZL, 5WJ2 etc.). But requirement says antibody binder "doesn't need to match" a pdb_ids protein — it can still match. Hmm.

But wait — there's an important clue: the antibody should bind the antibody binder. In the experiment, they want to use an antibody for detection/pull-down. If the binder is GFP, then GFP would serve double duty? But GFP would interfere with FRET (donor/acceptor are also fluorescent). Actually the fusion already has donor and acceptor FPsMENU. Adding GFP might complicate FRET. So the antibody binder is probably not a fluorescent protein.

What about **DHFR**? If the antibody is anti-DHFR, the antibody binder = DHFR. "Most common variant" - e.g., there are two common variants of human DHFR? Hmm, DHFR has common variants but not exactly "most common variant".

Actually, let me reconsider: maybe the antibody was designed against a **well-known protein with a single predominant isoform** like:
- **GST** (glutathione S-transferase): GST has many classes; "most common variant" could be GST Pi (P1) or GST M1 (GSTM1 has a null variant, so "most common" might matter). GST tags are commonly used and commercial anti-GST antibodies exist.
- **MBP** (maltose binding protein): common fusion tag, anti-MBP antibodies exist.
- **GFP**: anti-GFP antibodies exist.

Let me use a completely different approach: BLAST via NCBI. The NCBI BLAST API (Blast.cgi) can BLAST a protein sequence against `pdb` database, returning matches. If the antibody sequence matches an antibody structure in PDB, its title/description will show the antigen.

Let me try NCBI BLAST REST API:
1. POST to `https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Blast.cgi` with parameters: CMD=Put, PROGRAM=blastp, DATABASE=pdb, QUERY=<sequence>, EXPECT=..., etc.
2. Get RID, then poll with CMD=Get to retrieve results.

This is doable. Let me write a script to BLAST the heavy chain variable region (or full) against the pdb database.

Actually, blastp against "pdb" database might not include antibody constant regions. But it will match antibody structures in the PDB. The titles of the matches will tell us what antigen the antibody was against. 

Let me do this.
Let me use NCBI BLAST against the PDB to identify the antibody structure and hence its target antigen.




[tool-911] exec_command <- BLAST antibody heavy chain against PDB via NCBI ($ cd /tmp && python3 << 'EOF' import urllib.request, urllib.parse, time, re seq = "QVQLQQSAAELARPGASVKMSCKASGYSFTTYTIHWVKQRPGQGLEWIGYINPSSGYAAYNQNFKDETTLTADPSSSTAYMELNSLTSEDSAVYYCAREKFYGYDYWGQGATLTVSSASTTPPSVYPLAPGSAAQTNSMVTLGCLVKGYFPEPVTVTWNSGSLSSGVHTFPAVLQSDLYTLSSSVTVPSSTWPSQTVTCNVAHPASSTKVDKKIVPR" params = { 'CMD': 'Put', 'PROGRAM': 'blastp', 'DATABASE': 'pdb', 'QUERY': '>heavy\n' + seq, 'EXPECT': '1e-5', 'MATRIX': 'BLOSUM62', 'HITLIST_SIZE': 10, 'ALIGNMENTS': 3, 'FORMAT_TYPE': 'JSON2', 'FILTER': 'F', } data = urllib.parse.urlencode(params).encode() req = urllib.request.Request("https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Blast.cgi", data=data) resp = urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=90) body = resp.read().decode() m = re.search(r'RID = (\S+)', body) or re.search(r'"RID"\s*:\s*"(\S+)"', body) print(body[:1000]) print("RID:", m.group(1) if m else None) open('/tmp/rid.txt','w').write(m.group(1) if m else '') EOF)

BLAST has been submitted, RID = 954JMH3P016. Let me poll for results. The results from the RID. Let me write a polling script. Usually wait ~10-20 seconds. Let me poll with CMD=Get and FORMAT_TYPE=JSON2.
[tool-9b5] exec_command <- Poll NCBI BLAST results ($ cd /tmp && python3 << 'EOF' import urllib.request, urllib.parse, time, json, re rid = open('/tmp/rid.txt').read().strip() url = "https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Blast.cgi" for i in range(20): params = {'CMD':'Get','RID':rid,'FORMAT_TYPE':'JSON2','ALIGNMENTS':3} req = urllib.request.Request(url+"?"+urllib.parse.urlencode(params)) try: resp = urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=60) body = resp.read().decode() except Exception as e: print(e); time.sleep(10); continue # Check status try: j = json.loads(body) except Exception as e: print("parse err", e); print(body[:500]); time.sleep(10); continue st = j.get('BlastOutput',{}).get('program','') if 'Please wait' in body or 'no hits' in body.lower(): print('waiting...', body[:200]) time.sleep(10); continue # Save result open('/tmp/blast_result.json','w').write(body) print("got result size", len(body)) break time.sleep(10) EOF echo "---"; ls -la /tmp/blast_result.json 2>/dev/null && python3 -c " import json j=json.load(open('/tmp/blast_result.json')) for hit in j['BlastOutput']['report']['results']['search']['hits']: print(hit['description'][0]['title']) for hsp in hit['hsps'][:1]: print(' ', hsp['identity'], hsp['positive']) ")

[tool-673] exec_command <- Submit and poll BLAST robustly ($ cd /tmp && python3 << 'EOF' import urllib.request, urllib.parse, time, re, json, html seq = "QVQLQQSAAELARPGASVKMSCKASGYSFTTYTIHWVKQRPGQGLEWIGYINPSSGYAAYNQNFKDETTLTADPSSSTAYMELNSLTSEDSAVYYCAREKFYGYDYWGQGATLTVSSASTTPPSVYPLAPGSAAQTNSMVTLGCLVKGYFPEPVTVTWNSGSLSSGVHTFPAVLQSDLYTLSSSVTVPSSTWPSQTVTCNVAHPASSTKVDKKIVPR" def do_url(url, params=None, method='POST'): data = urllib.parse.urlencode(params).encode() if params else None req = urllib.request.Request(url, data=data, method=method, headers={'User-Agent':'Mozilla/5.0'}) return urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=60).read().decode() base = "https://blast.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/Blast.cgi" # Submit body = do_url(base, {'CMD':'Put','PROGRAM':'blastp','DATABASE':'pdb','QUERY':'>h\n'+seq,'EXPECT':'1e-5','HITLIST_SIZE':50,'ALIGNMENTS':0,'DESCRIPTIONS':50,'FORMAT_TYPE':'Text'}) m = re.search(r'RID = (\S+)', body) if not m: print("no RID in:", body[:500]); raise SystemExit rid = m.group(1) print("RID:", rid) for i in range(30): try: res = do_url(base + "?" + urllib.parse.urlencode({'CMD':'Get','RID':rid,'FORMAT_TYPE':'Text','ALIGNMENTS':0,'DESCRIPTIONS':50}), method='GET') except Exception as e: print("err", e); time.sleep(5); continue if 'Status: WAITING' in res or 'Status: QUEUED' in res or 'Searching...' in res or 'Please wait' in res: print(f"waiting {i}...") time.sleep(5) continue open('blast_out.txt','w').write(res) print("=== DONE len", len(res), "===") print(res[:3000]) break EOF)

Excellent!! The top two hits with score 1.0 are:

- **7BG1**: "Structure of anti-FLAG M2 Fab domain remodeled based on proteomic sequencing"
- **8RMO**: "Crystal structure of anti-FLAG M2 Fab fragment bound to FLAG-tag peptide epitope"

Both are **anti-FLAG M2 antibody**! So the antibody in antibody.fasta is the **anti-FLAG M2** antibody, and its target (antigen) is the **FLAG-tag peptide** (the FLAG epitope). 

So the antibody binder should encode the **FLAG-tag peptide** (the protein for which the antibody was designed). The FLAG epitope sequence is typically **DYKDDDDK** (Asp-Tyr-Lys-Asp-Asp-Asp-Asp-Lys). That's the "FLAG tag" peptide.

"Only encode the most common variant of that protein sequence, don't repeat the protein multiple times even if it increases binding affinity." Hmm, "most common variant" - the FLAG tag has a common variant: the standard FLAG epitope DYKDDDDK, and there is a "3xFLAG" (3xFLAG tag = DYKDHDGDYKDHDIDYKDDDDK) which has 3 repeats. "Don't repeat the protein multiple times even if it increases binding affinity" — this makes sense! The **3xFLAG** repeats the FLAG epitope 3 times to increase binding affinity to the anti-FLAG antibody. The task says: encode the **most common variant** (single FLAG tag: DYKDDDDK), do NOT repeat it (don't use 3xFLAG). That fits perfectly!

So the antibody binder = **FLAG tag** peptide with sequence **DYKDDDDK** (8 amino acids, but common variants exist). "Most common variant" of FLAG tag: The original/classic FLAG epitope is DYKDDDDK. 

Let me confirm: The anti-FLAG M2 antibody binds the FLAG epitope DYKDDDDK. The single FLAG tag is the most common variant apekso. The 3xFLAG is a repeated version. So antibody binder sequence = DYKDDDDK.

Now let me assemble the full design:

Fusion protein N→C order:
1. **Antibody binder (FLAG tag)**: DYKDDDDK (8 aa). Remove N-terminal Met (none present).
2. GS linker (5-20 aa)
3. **Donor (Clover / 5WJ2)**: sequence from PDB FASTA for 5WJ2. Remove N-terminal Met.
4. GS linker
5. **DHFR** from plasmid.gb translation. Remove N-terminal Met.
6. GS linker
7. **Acceptor (mCherry / 2H5Q)**: sequence from PDB FASTA for 2H5Q. Remove N-terminal Met.
8. GS linker
9. **Molecule binder (SNAP-tag / 3KZY)**: sequence from PDB FASTA for 3KZY. Remove N-terminal Met.

Wait — I need to reconsider the donor/acceptor order. "The order of the subproteins from N to C terminus should be: antibody binder - donor - dhfr - acceptor - molecule binder." Yes: antibody binder (FLAG), donor (Clover), DHFR, acceptor (mCherry), molecule binder (SNAP-tag).

But wait — "The acceptor and donor proteins should only be separated by DHFR and GS linkers." Yes, in this order donor and acceptor are separated by DHFR and linkers. Good.

Now get the protein sequences from the PDB API FASTA for each:
- 5WJ2 (Clover donor): The FASTA from earlier: `MGSSHHHHHHSSGENLYFQGHMVSKGEELFTGVVPILVELDGDVNGHKFSVRGEGEGDATNGKLTLKFICTTGKLPVPWPTLVTTFXVACFSRYPDHMKQHDFFKSAMPEGYVQERTISFKDDGTYKTRAEVKFEGDTLVNRIELKGIDFKEDGNILGHKLEYNFNSHNVYITADKQKNGIKANFKIRHNVEDGSVQLADHYQQNTPIGDGPVLLPDNHYLSHQSALSKDPNEKRDHMVLLEFVTAAGITHGMDELYK`
  This contains tags at N-terminus: MGSSHHHHHHSSGENLYFQGH then MVSK... starts the actual FP. Hmm. The task says "Their protein sequences should match the fasta file returned by the pdb API for the pdb id they encode." So we should use the exact sequence from the fasta file. But it has a His-tag and other tag residues: MGSSHHHHHHSSGENLYFQGHMVSK... Hmm, but the "protein" includes those tags? The instruction says "Their protein sequences should match the fasta file returned by the pdb API for the pdb id they encode." So we take the FASTA sequence as-is (the chain sequence). Now codon optimization: The hardest part is satisfying GC content 30-70% in every 50-nt window. Let me plan the codon optimization.

Total protein AA ≈ 259 + 235 + 158 + 207 + 8 + 4×15 = 927 aa → 2781 nt. Good, < 3000.

Codon optimization approach:
- Use a codon table (e.g., E. coli? The plasmid uses T7 promoter for E. coli expression, but the codon table should just satisfy GC constraints. Since GC constraints are 30-70%, we can use a generic codon table with all synonymous codons (excluding X residue, stop).
- Greedy local optimization: For each amino acid, choose a codon from its synonymous set that keeps the sliding window GC content within [30%,70%] everywhere, considering the full sequence.

Since we have freedom, a simple approach:
1. Compute for each amino acid the set of codons with their GC count (0,1,2,3 G/C).
2. Use a small lookahead greedy: process each codon; maintain the last 50 nt window. For the current position, choose a codon (preferring one with GC content that keeps the window in range). 

Because 50 nt window = ~16-17 codons, and we need every window in range, a greedy that only looks at current window may fail. A more robust approach: use dynamic programming / backtracking with the constraint that each 50-nt window has GC 15-35 out of 50.

Since total length ~2800 nt, we have ~933 codons. We can do a simple "sliding window" optimization: For each window, compute GC count; if out of range, adjust codons inside the window. Repeat until converged. Since we have many choices per codon (average ~3), this should converge.

Alternatively, we can use a different tactic: Choose codons to make the sequence have ~50% GC uniformly. If we select codons with GC content per codon ≈ half, windows will hover around 50%. But the protein sequence amino acid composition might bias GC. With a GC-rich amino acid composition (Gly, Ala, Pro), we might need to choose GC-poor codons and vice versa.

Let me just write a robust optimizer with a well-known algorithm:
- Use the "codon optimization with GC sliding window" via simulated annealing or a simple iterative repair:
  1. Start with a random codon assignment.
  2. For each 50-nt window, if GC count < 15 or > 35, find codons inside window that can change to fix it (choose codons with appropriate GC counts), and reassign.
  3. Repeat until all windows OK.

Since random assignment might need many repairs, let me instead do a direct construction:
- Precompute for each amino acid the list of codons (with GC content and maybe weights to prefer common codons).
- Use a greedy algorithm with backtracking at the level of codons within a sliding window.

Actually, given the moderate length (~930 codons), a simple approach: use dynamic programming with a state = last 49 nt of the sequence? State space huge. Better: use a "window-aware greedy" per codon:

For position i, we have the previous 49 nt (the last up to 49 nt before the codon). We choose a codon c such that for every 50-nt window that ends at position i+2 (fully covering this codon) and starts within the current codon, the GC count stays in [15,35]. Since windows that include the current codon are limited (at most 1 window if we process left to right), this reduces to: the window ending at the current position must have GC in range. Wait, windows are 50 nt; as we extend rightward, each new codon participates in windows that END after it. When we're choosing codon at position i, the windows that END at i+2, i+3... up to i+49 could include it. But we can only control the window ending at i+2 (fully inside the codon). Later windows depend on future codons too.fonts.

A simpler robust method: iterative repair is finehaystack. Let me implement:

1. Assign initial codons: for each amino acid, pick a "balanced" codon (aiming GC ≈ half). E.g., use a static codon table with a preferred codon per aa that has ~2 G/C when possible.
2. Compute all 50-nt windows GC counts.
3. Find violating windows, and for each, pick a codon inside that can be changed (prefer one in the "wrong GC direction") to push the window into range. E.g., if window GC too high (GC > 35), change a codon with high GC to one with lower GC (same aa). If too low (GC < 15), increase.
4. Repeat until no violations.

Because each codon change affects ~17 windows, this could oscillate. But with many codons and flexibility, it should converge.

To make it converge faster, I can bias codon selection initially to be GC-balanced (choose codons with GC count 0,1,2,3 such that the average approaches half).

GC content of a codon = number of G/C bases.

The sliding window: any 50 consecutive nucleotides. GC count must be between 15 and 35 (since 30% of 50 = 15, 70% = 35).

Let me implement the optimizer:

Approach:
1. Build the amino acid sequence.
2. Codon assignment via a simple strategy: for each amino acid, pick a codon with GC count closest to 2 (balanced). But that might not satisfy windows. Let's do iterative repair.

Iterative repair algorithm:
- Represent the DNA as a list of codons.
- Compute GC count for every window of size 50.
- For each violating window (GC < 15 or > 35), try to fix it by changing codons inside the window.
  - If GC < 15 (too low): find codons in the window that can be increased in GC (synonymous codon with higher GC count) without breaking other windows too much.
  - If GC > 35 (too high): find codons that can be decreased.
- Repeat until stable.

To make it deterministic and convergent, I'll implement a per-window slack check and a priority-based repair. But simpler: use a randomized search (simulated annealing) that flips codons to reduce a penalty function = sum over windows of violation amount. Since it's a local search, it should find a solution because there are many degrees of freedom.

Let me define penalty(w) = max(0, 15 - gc_w) + max(0, gc_w - 35) for each window w. Total penalty = sum. We want penalty = 0.

Local search: repeatedly pick a window with penalty > 0 magazine, pick a codon inside it, try changing to a synonymous codon (different GC) that reduces the total penalty. If it reduces, accept. If not accept with small probability (or skip). This is like hill climbing; should work.

Alternatively, a deterministic greedy from left to right with lookahead of 50 nt:
Processing left to right: when we place codon i, we ensure the window ending at position (i*3 + 2) [i.e., last codon fully included] has GC in range. But that doesn't guarantee windows that start before and extend beyond. Actually, the window constraint for windows ending at position p (p from 49 to L-1) — when we place codon i, windows ending at positions (i*3+2) ... are determined once we have the last 49 characters. If we enforce the window ending at the end of codon i, that ensures the window containing the current codon and previous 47 nt is valid. But a window that starts in the middle of codon i-1 extends past codon i; it would be enforced when we place codon i+1 etc. So greedy left-to-right with ensuring the window ending at the current codon's end is valid should suffice IF we also handle the initial boundary windows.

Let me think: A 50-nt window [s, s+49]. When we place the codon ending at position s+49, we can verify the window starting at s. As we process left to right, when we are about to place codon i (positions 3i..3i+2), the windows that END at 3i+2 have start = 3i+2-49 = 3i-47. This window spans codons i-16 to i (roughly). All codons i-16..i-1 are already placed (if i > 16), and codon i is being placed. So we can compute the required GC for codon i to make this window valid — but codon i contributes 0-3 GC bases; if the required GC for the window is achievable, we can choose. However, subsequent windows that end later also depend on codon iholhen; they'll be checked when placing later codons. Since each future window includes codon i, if we choose codon i with GC k, then future windows with start s' in [3i-47, 3i] will need appropriate future codons. This greedy may not guarantee feasibility for far windows (e.g., a codon with fixed GC could constrain many windows). 

But with ~17 codons per window and flexibility in most positions, a greedy local approach usually works if we don't hit edge cases. Let me implement both: greedy with fallback repair.

Actually simplest robust: implement **simulated annealing / random walk + local search** on codons. Given ~933 codons each with 1-6 synonyms, the search space is huge but the constraint should be satisfiable. A random walk editing codons to reduce penalty should converge.

Let me just implement a straightforward iterative improvement:

```
def total_penalty(dna):
    gc = [0/1 for each base]
    windows GC counts
    return sum(penalties)

codons = initial assignment (balanced)
for iteration in range(N):
    find windows with penalty > 0
    pick a random violating window
    pick a random codon inside it with alternatives
    try each alternative, compute new penalty for affected windows, accept if improves (best improvement)
```

To speed, compute window GC counts incrementally. For a single codon change, only windows overlapping that codon change (~17 windows) need recompute.
